->TO THE KEPLERIAN APPENDIX.<-

urges that they should be cast aside. Good God, how great is the sharpness of intellect in this man! He has crept into and penetrated the very marrow of the teachings of such great men, after so many centuries have passed, just to observe their mistakes, so that he might finally purge the world of errors that have remained hidden for so long. Or perhaps the speculation of the ancient world was so crude that, before this time, it was unable to look into the errors of the Ancients in Astronomy and Music.

But let us now return to his own self-love <note>original: "philautiam," from the Greek *philautia*, meaning excessive conceit or vanity.</note> and his vast inspection of nature. We find two impressions of this matter in this context; the first is: *I draw the material of my harmony from the nature of things, and establish it from its very foundations.* The second: *I proceed in a natural order, so that all things are corrected according to the laws of nature, and confusion is avoided; and thus I do not even apply established rules to the opinions of the Ancients, except where no confusion follows.* Since he boasts that in his books on the *Harmony of the World* he has opened the sanctuary of nature, and has found in it the true and natural harmony of things above all others—extracting it from the very womb and foundations of nature—it will certainly be right to leaf through his books. From there, we may gather whether such a revelation of nature was actually made there or not; and similarly, whether he proceeds by a method and order so natural as to correct the "confusions" of other Authors.

He distributes his **first book** into geometric definitions and propositions concerning multifaceted surfaces with equal sides, which he calls "regular." With these, he makes a more ready entry into the composition of his "regular solids," from whose proportions he seems to elicit his harmony. These plane figures, which he treats in *this book*, he makes either perfectly regular (in which all sides and angles are equal); and these he divides again into "primary and radical" figures, or "augmented" ones—those which exceed their sides when the non-adjacent sides of some radical figure are continued until they meet. These he calls "stars" <note>This refers to Kepler's discovery of star polygons, now known as Kepler-Poinsot polyhedra.</note>. Or he calls them less perfect or "semi-regular," which vary in their angles even if their sides are equal, such as Rhombs. Here he reviews many degrees of knowledge pertaining to angular figures. 

He also has many propositions pertaining to the sides of the Square, the Octagon, the Sixteen-sided figure, the Triangle, the Hexagon, the Heptagon, the Dodecagon, and its star; the Decagon with its star, the Pentagon with its star, and other such regular angular figures. Similarly, he treats the circle and its various sections, and here he ends that book. In it, we find nothing pertaining to the true Harmony of the world’s nature; rather, he makes a certain Geometric comparison between one angular figure and another. If this indeed be harmony, it is certainly "analogical" and named so by resemblance, and not in truth—or rather, it is a Mathematical and imaginary Harmony, not a Natural one, which consists of the benevolent interactions and perfectly disposed passions of the world-soul <note>term: "anima mundana" (World Soul). In Fludd's philosophy, this is the spiritual force that animates the universe.</note> with its material substance. It was never the intention of the Philosophers that natural harmony should consist of Geometric dimensions, since the acting soul does not admit of a visible measurement.

In the **second book**, he treats the "congruence" <note>term: "congruentia" (congruence). The way shapes fit together to fill a space without gaps.</note> of figures, which he applies either to a plane—making this either "perfect," when the individual angles of multiple surfaces meet at a point so that no gap is left and all that meet are similar (this being a congruence of figures of the same species); or "imperfect," where there is a similar mixture of figures of different species. Or he applies it to solids. This is "most perfect" when the angles of figures (whether regular or semi-regular) of the same species meet; or "perfect but of a lower degree" when figures fitting at the angles are of different species; or it is an "imperfect congruence" when, other things remaining the same, a larger figure is found no more than once or twice. In planes, therefore, he says the Triangle fits with the square, and likewise the pentagon with its star; Hexagons also fit together; so too the Octagon and Decagon with their stars, and the Dodecagon with its star, etc. In semi-regular bodies, he describes congruence in *Propositions 27 and 28*. Regarding the most perfectly regular ones, he treats them in *Propositions 25 and 26*. In *Proposition 25*, he describes those five "most perfect and regular bodies" <note>These are the five Platonic Solids: the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note>, about whose properties the Pythagoreans, Plato, and Proclus sang so much. And here he believes the force of the world’s harmony consists; and so this is the force of his *Book 2*. Here we see that this harmony of his, which he calls the "congruence of figures," is entirely imaginary; he interprets Plato’s intention through a mystical allusion to those five regular bodies.

In the **third book**, he treats first the discovery of Music, Pythagorean numbers, and the Pythagorean Tetractys <note>The "Tetractys" is a triangular figure consisting of ten points, a sacred symbol for Pythagoreans representing the organization of space and the universe.</note> through definitions, axioms, and many propositions for inquiring into the causes of the "delays" <note>likely referring to rhythmic durations or rests</note> of musical consonances. Similarly, he treats the discovery of harmonic proportions from various sections of a string. He also treats harmonic means, the origin of suitable intervals (where he strives to refute the opinions of the Ancients), the division of consonant intervals into suitable ones, the types of Song (Soft and Hard <note>note: "Molle & Durum." These terms refer to the hexachord system; "Molle" (soft) used B-flat, while "Durum" (hard) used B-natural, roughly correlating to minor and major qualities.</note>), the number of intervals in the Octave consonance <note>original: "Diapason"</note>, the division of the Monochord, the composition of systems, "adulterine" <note>meaning false or impure</note> consonances, naturally suitable Song, the modes of melodies, and other such things. Thus, in *this book*, we find nothing except mere "artificial music" gathered from the books of many Ancient Authors; we find nothing of his own here, except that he delights in contradicting the Ancients' reasons for the discovery of intervals.

In these *first three books*, therefore, the Author has neither drawn the material of his harmony from the nature of things, nor established it from its foundations, as he promised; nor has he proceeded naturally so that anything might be corrected by the laws of nature to avoid confusion. Let us proceed, therefore, to his **fourth book**, in which, before he arrives at the full flow of his subject,

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critical analysis of Johannes Kepler’s Harmonices Mundi (Harmonies of the World). Fludd is specifically summarizing the arguments of Kepler's Fourth Book before preparing to dismantle them. He focuses on Kepler's distinction between "sensible" harmony (perceived by the ears) and "archetypal" or "pure" harmony (the mathematical patterns underlying the universe).</meta>

### ANALYTICAL DISCOURSE

...comes to this, he divides Harmony into that which is sensible, and that which is secret and pure, removed from sensible things. For the consistency and being of the former, he says four things must concur: namely, two sensible things of the same genus and quantity; a Soul that compares or distinguishes them (or the reception of sensible things); and a suitable proportion, which he calls Harmony. Thus, he recounts there in many words that a "numbering soul" <note>original: "Anima numerans"</note> is required for the existence of sensible Harmony; without this soul, number is taken away. He also teaches how external things are received into the soul and explains the degrees of actions and passions. He further teaches that this kind of harmony is, in a certain way, abstracted from things.

The other species of harmony, which he calls secret, pure, and insensible, he identifies as the Archetypes or Paradigms <note>Archetypes are the original "blueprints" or divine patterns upon which reality is modeled.</note> of sensible harmonies. He maintains that these Archetypal harmonies are divided and many in number, and says that two "terms" are required for them: namely, either a whole circle or some part or parts of it. Here, therefore, he seems to remove all harmony except for circular harmony. Next, besides these terms, he also requires a Mind for the existence of such harmonies; and there he describes how the species of mathematical things exist within the soul. He brings forth the differences between **Aristotle**, **Plato**, and **Proclus** on this matter, along with their doctrines and other such things that prove a relationship between this mathematical harmony of his and the mind or soul. 

He then proceeds to form the faculties of the soul according to these harmonies. And finally, he falls upon the proposed subject of this book, which seems to pertain to what he calls secret and pure harmony. He wants the same proportions found in sensible harmony to concur in this kind of harmony as well, though they differ in name. For what is called the *Diapason* <term>the Octave</term> in sounds, he wants to be called an *Opposition* in the radiations of planetary aspects. Similarly, he compares vocal Music with that of the world, in that just as in a musical system a human voice or the sound of instruments is either higher or lower, or sharper or deeper, so too is Meteorology manifold: it arises either from the conjunction of Saturn, or of Jupiter, or from another pairing, or from the equinoctial or tropical signs. 

This celestial and insensible harmony, which he treats here, he says belongs to angles, which consist in motion just as sensible harmony consists in sound. For he says these harmonies reside in the angles which two planets, by emitting rays of light, form upon the earth. Therefore, he makes this "world harmony" a mixture in respect to that "truly celestial" one (as he says) created on earth by the two rays of two planets. 

Then he proceeds to the causes of the efficacy of these configurations, where he first deals with the angles that two planets make on the earth by their radiations. Likewise, he treats the kinship and congruence of these radiations, and then the degrees of the aspects. He makes the first degree the **Conjunction** and **Opposition**; the second, the **Square**; the third, the **Trine**, **Sextile**, and **Semi-sextile**; the fourth, the **Quintile**, **Biquintile**, and **Quincunx**; and the fifth and last—which he makes the weakest of all—the **Decile**, **Tredecile**, **Octile**, and **Trioctile**. 

He therefore makes the **Sextile** correspond to the soft Third <note>A minor third.</note> in consonance; the **Quintile** to the hard Third <note>A major third.</note>; the **Square** to the *Diatessaron* <term>the Fourth</term>; the **Trine** to the *Diapente* <term>the Fifth</term>; the **Sesquadrate** to the soft Sixth; the **Biquintile** to the hard Sixth; and the **Opposition** to the *Diapason* <term>the Octave</term>. 

By the power of these aspects making various angles on the earth, he says that—because of a certain "peculiar soul"—vapors, smoke, and exhalations, as well as storms and "meteors" <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning.</note> (whether watery, windy, or fiery), are excited in the air. He claims that natural changes occur in the waters and the earth itself, which he has therefore dared to call an **animal**. This is indeed the force of his fourth book, in which he seems to touch upon one species of natural harmony as if with a needle. 

Let us see, therefore, whether in this matter his harmony agrees so much with the truth of the thing, and whether he draws it from the fountain of natural things or not; and whether he proceeds by the order of nature so that confusion might be avoided. As far as the harmony of radiations is concerned, let us inquire whether that most exact order of nature is in it—the order by which he boasted he would avoid confusion and correct what was uncorrected.

First, it seems likely that the radiation of two planets making angles on the earth is not the cause of storms or changes in the air, but rather that it is a certain occult and admirable power of influence, bending the air here and there according to its own nature and appetite. Hence the wise man said not inaptly that <note>marginal reference to Ecclesiastes 1:6</note> *the air, circling by the conversation of the sun, hurries along and returns according to its circuits.* From this it is clear that whatever movements of the air exist, they are not excited for the sake of some peculiar, imaginary "Soul of the Earth" by reason of angles made on the earth to promote storms. Rather, they are excited because of the Solar spirit, or rather the virtues of Wisdom acting everywhere, according to the dispositions of the same in the heavens toward other planets varying in nature and motion, to which that spirit gives light, life, and soul. 

Whence the power of radiations is chiefly in the air, as if in a lower spirit of the world-soul or a receptacle for its act, prone to this or that disposition of working—whether fiery, watery, or windy. For the soul and life of the earth is rather *from* the air and *in* the air, than that the movements of the air (whether natural or accidental) should proceed *from* the earth. For the air gives life to the waters, life to the earth, and life to all things composed of them. 

We also see winds born in the air (though whence they come is very difficult to know, according to the word of the Scriptures, even if **Aristotle** makes them the "sighs of the earth"). We see them, I say, afflict the seas in various ways, stir up storms within them, and violently tear down mountains, rocks, and trees of the earth. Is it characteristic of an "earthly soul" to be so cruelly opposed to itself, and to destroy and tear apart its own members? Rains, snows, thunders, and Comets (drawn from the treasuries of God according to the doctrine of the Sacred Bible) are also produced and arise in the air. It escapes no one how much slaughter and ruin they stir up upon the earth, as well as upon the sea. Therefore, it would be a foul thing if damages of this kind to the earth were derived from its own soul; but rather they come from the universal [soul] of the world...

<meta>The catchword "mundi" (of the world) at the bottom indicates the start of the next page's first sentence.</meta>

<summary>Robert Fludd summarizes Kepler's Fourth Book, detailing Kepler's theory that planetary angles (aspects) trigger a "Soul of the Earth" to create weather. Fludd begins his rebuttal, arguing that the air and solar spirit are the true drivers of these phenomena, mocking the idea that the Earth possesses a soul that would destructively attack its own "body" with storms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critique of Johannes Kepler's *Harmonices Mundi*. On the previous page, Fludd summarized Kepler's view that a "numbering soul" is required to perceive harmony. Here, Fludd begins to argue that Kepler's reliance on purely mathematical angles is insufficient to explain the physical and spiritual "inflow" of the heavens into the sublunary world.</meta>

# TO THE KEPLERIAN APPENDIX

### B 3

...from that soul of the world <note>original: "mundi anima"</note> these things proceed, inasmuch as it is accustomed to swim and act abundantly in its aerial vehicle, which serves as a passive medium best suited for its actions. This is even more the case if a likeness of itself rains down from the heavens; indeed, this influence is stronger and more diverse in its action when it is fortified by two views and radiations forming an angle in the air (which is the common receptacle of celestial inflows). And then the earth itself, which continually exhales a magnetic force, receives that spirit and sometimes retains it for a long time, insofar as all its empty pores are filled with air. It retains this spirit like a seed in a woman’s womb, where, according to the powers of the influences, various things are procreated in the womb of the earth—and indeed upon its surface. Do we not see even in marble, the hardest and firmest part of the earth, that the air itself, burdened with moist influences, penetrates that compact body? And that its internal salt, dissolved by this moisture, emits copious "sweats"—signs of future rains? Do we not sometimes see the earth itself as if choked and submerged by an abundance of rain, laboring as if from dropsy <note>A historical term for edema or swelling; here, Fludd treats the earth like a living body suffering from an excess of fluid.</note>? From where comes such a great quantity of water, if not from the air, since the earth cannot give more than it has? Nor does it proceed from the sea, because its size is not diminished. To produce Noah’s flood, not only was the abyss of the earth dilated, but the cataracts and windows of heaven were also opened. Moreover, we have shown in our *Macrocosmic Treatise* <note>Fludd refers to his own massive work, *Utriusque Cosmi ... Historia* (The History of the Two Worlds).</note> that clouds and rains are produced from the condensation of the air.

We conclude, therefore, that in the lower realm of the world, air is the **spirit of life**, whose soul is fire, and consequently, it is the immediate receptor of whatever celestial influences and radiations there may be, which it afterward pours into the bowels of the earth. And hence the wiser Philosophers did not foolishly make fire and air the *acting* elements, while water and earth are the *passive* ones. Thus, it would be a grave error to believe that the passive elements act upon the active ones more than the active upon the passive. Truly, these angles of radiation are not "mathematical," as the Author <note>Fludd refers to Kepler as "the Author."</note> wishes, but physical, resembling corporeal or truly substantial pyramids; insofar as two lights possess the aerial spirit from the base all the way to the cone, not otherwise than we see in the flame of a fire or a candle. We say, therefore, that in the beginning *the Spirit of the Lord, which was the soul of the whole, moved and was carried over the waters, and not in the darkness or the earth* <note>A paraphrase of Genesis 1:2: "The Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters."</note>. From this, it is probable that the angles of the visible rays were made in the air, and not in the earth. We shall examine his opinions concerning the earth a little further below; but for now, let us proceed to the natures and harmonies of the radiations.

I perceive that he exceeds the limits of the ancient Philosophers in the number of radiations, so that he might drag the business of his "aspects" <note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angle between planets as seen from Earth.</note> by the hair, as it were, toward the intent of his Harmony. The ancients recognized only the **Conjunction, Sextile, Square, Trine, and Opposition**. He adds the **Semisextile, Quintile, Biquintile, and Quincunx**; indeed, even the **Decile, Tredecile, Bisextile, and Trisextile**; so that he seems to establish more radiations than there are integral parts in his Archetypal Zodiac, to which, according to his opinion, these aspects are dedicated and assigned. But he said this so that he might make a comparison between the regular mathematical bodies and these radiations, so that in this way he might attribute to them the same harmonic proportions and consonances that he assigned to the others.

He gives the **Diapason** <note>The "Diapason" is the musical interval of an Octave (2:1).</note>, therefore, to the aspect of **Opposition**. This, it seems to me, is incongruously done; because the Diapason is the most complete, most perfect, and most consonant of all harmonies. Yet, it is proven by the effect how lethal and violent the actions of this aspect are here on earth; for in the opposition of the Moon with the Sun—and indeed of other planets, especially the malefic ones <note>Fludd is referring to Saturn and Mars, traditionally considered "malefic" or ill-omened planets in astrology.</note>—we perceive the greatest danger of disease to be imminent, and the bells in the churches are by no means silent—which is common knowledge in places suffering from the plague. Hence, this aspect should be estimated as a place of dissonance and antipathy rather than the most perfect consonance and sympathy. The other consonances, when compared to the aspects and subjected to proper examination, will have no better flavor. Indeed, we do not think that Astrologers need more than five radiations; for from their diligent observation, the greater changes of the world may be elicited. It would be too scrupulous to track and consider fractions and minutiae in which the discrepancy of things is so small and obscure, when it is certain that those meanings are included and contained within the major ones.

But truly, to descend to his opinions about the earth, on **page 158**, he says: *Since the rays of the Planets always meet in the earth, rains then fall more readily if the rays of two Planets make angles of 60 degrees rather than 59 or 63.* I respond that the rays of the Planets meet first in the air, where they receive their mixtures, and not in the earth. Hence, clouds are generated in the air before rain falls into the womb of the earth for the sake of bringing about fertility; nor does the vapor of a cloud arise from the earth. On **page 160**, he says: *There must be a Soul which, warned and as if excited by an aspect, stirs up meteors and tempests, etc.* It follows, therefore, that this soul, which stirs the air according to the prescribed aspect, is here on earth. We respond that there is not any peculiar soul—and much less one belonging to the earth, which is most friendly to rest and consequently contrary to the action of a soul—but rather a single **Soul of the World**, which, swimming invisibly in the elements and the aerial spirit as if in its own vehicle, brings about alterations. For just as man, the microcosm, has only a single soul to direct his body, so also the Macrocosm is governed by that single divine spirit, which was carried over the waters, exhibiting a fiery power to them universally. For the soul of the air—which is no more divided from that of the Sun than a ray is from its body—produces various effects by celestial instinct, no differently than the heaven does by an Archetypal, divine, and ideal influence; for the word "heaven" corresponds to both the air and the ether. On **page 160**, he says: *Since the matter of rains, winds, mists, thunders, and chasms is elicited at the time of the aspects...*

<summary>Fludd rejects Kepler's mathematical and earth-centered view of planetary aspects, arguing instead that celestial influences act through the World Soul via the air. He critiques Kepler's invention of new astrological aspects and his assignment of the perfect musical octave to the hostile aspect of opposition.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s detailed rebuttal of Johannes Kepler’s "Harmonices Mundi" (The Harmony of the World). Fludd is arguing against Kepler’s naturalistic and mathematical explanation of weather and the nature of the Earth, favoring a more mystical, scriptural, and "vitalist" perspective where God acts directly through hidden "treasuries" in the air.</meta>

# ANALYTICAL DISCOURSE

...whether it be a moist vapor, or another dry and fiery spirit bubbling and exhaling from the earth, etc. This assertion of his we utterly deny: for we say that the matter of the aforementioned meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, snow, and wind, not just falling stars.</note> is in the most hidden bosom of the elements, or the divine treasury in the air—sometimes appearing visibly, but for the most part lying hidden. What need is there for the opinion of the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum"</note>, or those who describe the hidden natures of things by imagination alone and after the fact? I have read and re-read all their writings on this matter as well as those of wisdom—namely the Holy Bible—and in them I found more certain signs of these secrets of nature. For in Job 37:10 <note>OCR cites Job 27:9, but the text quotes 37:10: "By the breath of God frost is given."</note> I read, *The mighty God brought forth ice by his breath*; and Exodus 9, *Jehovah sent rain with hail upon the earth*; and Psalm 147, *God sends snow upon the earth like wool*, etc. As soon as He breathes out His wind, the waters flow. And Job 13: *Do we not know, when God disposes the clouds, how the light of His cloud shines?* And Job 38:22, *Have you entered into the treasuries of the snow, or have you seen the treasuries of the hail?* And verse 37, *Who has made the sapphire clouds with wisdom, and who can tip over the bottles of heaven?* And Job 36, *Will a man understand the differences or the spreads of the thick clouds, and the thunders or the crashes of the firmament, or the pavilion of Jehovah? God thunders wondrously with His voice. When He commands the snow, it is upon the earth; or to the shower and the rain, they descend upon the earth*, etc. Do you know where the way is in which the exhalation is divided, which scatters the wind over the earth? Similarly, when God sent His spirit so that the waters of the flood might be calmed and diminished, according to Genesis 8 <note>OCR cites Genesis 18</note>, did this wind proceed from the earth, when, by his own assertion, wind is a hot and dry exhalation? Surely this is impossible, because the entire dry force of the earth was submerged by the violence of the immense moisture; indeed, the very soul of the earth, which he calls a flame, would undoubtedly be submerged and suffocated by such a quantity of water, if any such thing existed. From these things it is clear that rains, showers, snow, hail, thunders, chasms <note>original: "chasmata," referring to atmospheric openings or flashes of light</note>, and winds are drawn out from the hidden treasuries of Jehovah by means of the Word, and not from the earth, as he himself foolishly opined. For either the Holy Scriptures are false, or his assertions are adulterated, since the Scripture testifies that the heaven is *the excellent treasury of Jehovah, which by opening He will give rain to the earth in its time* (Deuteronomy 28:12), and by closing it, *there may be no rain on the land to produce crops for the wicked* (Deuteronomy 11:17). Are not the *Winds* also called the *breath of the nostrils of nature* in Exodus 15:8? There are, therefore, so many and such great hidden mysteries of Jehovah in the air that it is impossible for human genius to recount them. Indeed, this air is His life or luminous soul with the spirit, which assidueously and continually penetrates it and makes it live. This air, when contracted, is nothing other than fire in water, or a thick soul agitated in such a spirit toward this or that movement—namely, fiery or watery, in manifold ways by the will of Jehovah.

->**Folio 160.**<-
*Finally, the terrestrial globe will be such a body as that of some animal; for as an animal has a soul, so also the earth has a soul, by the presence of whose passions it moves storms.*

I respond that a soul is present in the earth, as the "soul of the whole" is said to be in any of its parts; but not that it has a soul distinct from that of the universal world, which he himself placed in the spiritual sun, whose soul is Metattron <note>Metatron; in Hermetic and Kabbalistic thought, a high archangel often associated with the governing of the world or the celestial scribe.</note>. Furthermore, I would have him understand that the internal part of each thing is heaven itself, which operates by itself without the harmony of the Great Heaven. *So we see fire burn and act without any compulsion from celestial passions.*

->**Folio 160.**<-
*The earth is an animal, not like a dog ready at every nod, but like an elephant or a bustard* <note>original: "Bostardum," likely referring to the Great Bustard, a heavy bird known for its slow, deliberate movement.</note> *prone to anger.*

To this assertion of his, we say that he proves by this very point, against the opinion of Copernicus, that the Earth is not fit to be carried around in the space of 24 hours, since it is naturally so sluggish and slow to move. Finally, he runs off into many vanities to prove the earth is an animal through comparisons made between the hairs of an animal and grasses with plants; between lice and caterpillars and locusts; between tears and amber <term>electrum<gloss>a natural yellow resin (amber), which was often poetically described as the tears of trees</gloss></term>; between drinking and the sea's absorption of water. He grants hearing to the earth because a pebble thrown into its gasps or deep holes is accustomed to make a sound, not considering that this sound proceeds from the enclosed air; by whose hidden power it also happens that storms are said to be stirred up by the commotion of certain lakes. He even ascribes diseases and languors to the earth on account of "rawness" and the imperfection of its "digestion" <note>He is using medical terminology to describe geological processes</note>. To which we say, that its soul must be very bad and malevolent if it is accustomed to afflict its own body with such infirmities.

->**Folio 162.**<-
*He asserts that the ebb and flow of the sea proves the inhalation and exhalation of the earth.*

To which we say, the body of this animal must be miraculous, for it is accustomed to take in more water by "breathing in" at one time than its own size allows; for it is no small debate whether the sea or the earth itself is larger.

->**Folio 163.**<-
*In the earth, the soul (by which it seems to be nourished through breathing and swallowing) is evidence of a perpetual and sensible subterranean heat.*

We say that if this were truly the case, it would be a marvel how that flame could have resisted the invasions of the Great Flood. I acknowledge indeed that an *Archaeus* <note>In Paracelsian medicine, the "internal alchemist" or vital force that governs the functions of an organ or the earth.</term> in the center of the earth performs the works of nature there in its own way, and that there is a central sun which operates there and performs its duty; but that this sun or that *Archaeus* is a fiery flame I utterly deny, although "Typhonian" flames <note>Volcanic or sulfurous fires, named after the Greek monster Typhon.</note> are sometimes perceived to rise from the earth; these, however, are recognized by the Wise Philosopher to happen by a far different reason, just as flame comes from a thick cloud or sparks from a flint.

->**Folio 163.**<-
*God does not only have geometric patterns with Him, etc.*

We deny that lines or surfaces can be imagined in God, who is Unity; although number, weight, and measure were known to Him. But that measure does not pertain to vulgar Geometry, but rather to that purely formal Geometry which no one is permitted to know unless formal numbers and letters are known to them.

->**Folio 163.**<-
*Therefore, there shines in the soul of the earth a certain image of the sensible Zodiac circle, and indeed of the whole firmament—the bond of sympathy between celestial and terrestrial things.*

We also affirm that an imaginary, or if you prefer, an ideal figure of the Zodiac...

<meta>catchword: ...material</meta>

<summary>Fludd refutes Kepler's naturalistic view of weather and the "breathing" earth, arguing instead that meteorological phenomena come from God's hidden treasuries. He mocks Kepler's anthropomorphic comparisons of the Earth to an animal (likening grass to hair and amber to tears) and uses Kepler's own "sluggish" animal metaphor to argue against Copernican rotation.</summary>

<keywords>Robert Fludd, Johannes Kepler, meteorology, biblical cosmology, soul of the earth, Metatron, Archaeus, tides, geometry, Copernicanism, hidden treasuries, Job, Deuteronomy</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critique of Johannes Kepler. Here, Fludd transitions from discussing atmospheric theories to a direct attack on Kepler's "Harmonices Mundi," specifically challenging Kepler's use of the five Platonic solids and his defense of the Copernican heliocentric model. Fludd argues for the continued relevance of Ptolemy and the literal interpretation of the Bible regarding the immobility of the Earth.</meta>

->### TO THE KEPLERIAN APPENDIX.<-

...the images of heaven are imprinted on the stars and every other element just as they are on the earth. Therefore, since the idea of the Zodiac reaches the air before it reaches the earth itself, it is necessary that the air—because of its easy reception of these influences—be stirred by them first, either for good or for ill; and that it then pours those influences into the terrestrial matrix. Here, this air (which is *expanded fire* <note>original: "ignis expansus"</note> outside the earth and *contracted fire* <note>original: "ignis contractus"</note> within the earth) hastens to bring the infused seeds to maturity. And so it is with the rest. From which... <note>The text here seems to bridge a gap or refer back to those in need.</note> Is this not, then, to proceed by a natural order, to correct everything according to the laws of nature, and to avoid confusion?

But now we shall turn the thread of our discourse to his final book. There, he prepares those five Pythagorean bodies—namely the Cube, the Tetrahedron, the Dodecahedron, the Icosahedron, and the Octahedron <note>These are the five Platonic solids, which Kepler used in his model of the solar system to explain the distances between planets.</note>—and adapts them to the intervals of his celestial Harmony. For this reason, in the beginning, he deduces the kinship of harmonic proportions with those bodies: the first of which he makes from their characters in relation to each other; the second from their origin, such as 1:2, 2:3, 3:4, and 4:5; and the third from the numbers of the sides of the planes compared with the number of the sides of the figure, such as the Cubic proportion 4:12 (or 1:3), the Tetrahedral 3:6 (or 1:2), and so on. Furthermore, he compares the bodies among themselves, and afterwards approaches the essence of his harmonic subject. Since he included the chief subject of this in Chapter 3 of Book 5, we shall proceed to a brief analysis of it.

First, he persuades the Reader to utterly reject and remove from the mind and consideration the ancient Astronomical Hypotheses of Ptolemy, as they were explained by Purbach <note>Georg von Peuerbach (1423–1461), an astronomer whose work helped revive Ptolemaic astronomy in the Renaissance.</note> and others; and this for the reason that they do not provide the true disposition of the world's bodies or the proper governance of their motions. I respond that it is by no means fitting for a Wise man to give place to such persuasions rashly and without counsel. First, because we have never found anyone in the whole world more learned or more skilled in Astronomy than Ptolemy. Why then should we reject him to follow the new inventions of Copernicus, Tycho Brahe, and Kepler—wrested as they are from Pythagoras’s regular bodies and by no means rightly conceived according to his and Plato's opinions—unless a greater probability of truth declared that ancient doctrine to be erroneous, which we can even now gather from Kepler's writings?

The opinions of Ptolemy and of all ancient Astrologers and Astronomers—indeed, even of the moderns (with a few exceptions)—have approved of eccentric and concentric motion <term>Eccentric and concentric motion: Geometric models used in geocentric astronomy to explain why planets appear to change speed or distance while orbiting the Earth.</term> in their own way. For this reason, they established the Apogee <note>original: "Augem," referring to the point in an orbit farthest from the center.</note> and its opposite. They recognized the Apogee and the sphere carrying the Planet with its own axis and poles, and another sphere carrying the Planet itself with the axis and poles of the ecliptic; and they made one axis for the sphere carrying the Sun, and another for the world. To the other Planets, they assigned an Epicycle with its triple axis (namely of reflection, declination, and longitude), along with the triple motion of that Circle: Direct, Stationary, and Retrograde. Indeed, by these observations, they composed calculations of the motions of the stars and tables of approved certainty; they set forth learned Ephemerides <note>Tables providing the positions of celestial bodies at regular intervals.</note> that were most exact in the motions of the stars, through which they predicted changes in the weather excellently, even to the hour and minute. And certainly, if this doctrine of theirs is as spurious as Kepler seems to proclaim it to the world, it would be wonderful indeed that they could produce so many certainties in the observation of the stars' motions. Therefore, I advise the learned not to desert the foundations of Ptolemy rashly, lest by curiosity they acquire a mere trifle in place of something valuable by accepting things that are worse and of less importance, thus infecting more excellent things with the vice of ingratitude and the rust of neglect and oblivion.

Let us see, then, what the marrow of this doctrine may be. He makes the earth one of the Planets and says, with Copernicus, that it is carried among the stars around an immobile Sun. He confirms this opinion of Copernicus through the hypotheses of Tycho Brahe, asserting that the earth moves around the Sun by a real motion of its body, walking a middle path between the outer circles of Mars and the inner circles of Venus. I say first that it seems the Sun could by no means be immobile because of the abundance of its light, which is more mobile than all things. For the duty of a true form (such as light is) and of an *act* is, first, that it must necessarily act; and second, it is fitting that its actions follow motion, because the effect of an act is motion. Hence it is a marvel that the Sun, which moves all things (for he calls it the "source of motion" in many places), should be immobile, and thus that old axiom of the Philosophers is confounded: *That which makes something a certain way is itself more so.* <note>The principle that the cause of a quality must possess that quality to an even higher degree.</note> But if he understands the Sun to be God Himself, I do indeed grant it: for God alone is He who moves all things and is not moved, insofar as He is outside of all things. Otherwise, it is necessary that the Sun, since its form is the effect of a divine act, should move other things in the same way that it is itself moved by God; for it is a physical body, which is naturally mobile.

As for the Daily Motion of the Earth, nothing more absurd can be imagined. I have given sufficient arguments for this in my *Physical Treatise on the Macrocosm* where, in Chapter 15 of Book 5, I attempted to refute that opinion of Copernicus. Then, let whoever wishes follow those prophets Heraclides of Pontus, Ecphantus, Nicetas of Syracuse, Aristarchus of Samos, and finally Copernicus and Kepler. But I shall choose Job and King David, and Solomon himself for my prophets, who expressly forge this opinion: that the Sun is mobile, but the earth is immobile. When these things are rightly understood, the foundations of the Author’s harmony will be easily destroyed, and consequently, it will be perceived that he poorly extracts the matter of his harmony from the nature of things and from the foundations themselves...

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critical analysis of Johannes Kepler's "Harmonices Mundi" (The Harmony of the World). On the previous page, Fludd began discussing how Kepler uses geometry to define the structure of the heavens. This page moves into a specific critique of Kepler's heliocentric model and his use of the five Platonic solids to explain planetary distances, before shifting to a discussion on the nature of musical consonances.</meta>

# ANALYTICAL DISCOURSE

...of the mind. Then he says, it is most certain among everyone that all the Planets circle the Sun, except for the Moon, which alone has the Earth as its center. This is something I have not yet perceived to this day. For I have another Astronomy closer to me, which truly teaches me all these things: for I do not believe that all the celestial orbs, and the Earth itself along with them, rotate around the Sun; and I shall prove that holding the Sun as the center of the world is by no means consistent with the truth. Furthermore, he asserts that the eccentricity of a Planet is the change in its distance from the Sun, which he makes the center; and there he offers a visual demonstration of the proportions of the distances of one Planet from another. 

Then he argues that the Number of the Planets, or their paths around the Sun, was chosen by the All-wise Creator from the five regular solid figures. For instance, when the angles of a Cube are applied to the innermost circle of Saturn, the centers of its faces almost touch the middle circle of Jupiter; when the angles of a Tetrahedron <note>a pyramid with four triangular faces</note> are placed on the innermost circle of Jupiter, the centers of the Tetrahedron's faces almost touch the outermost circle of Mars. Similarly, when the angles of an Octahedron <note>eight triangular faces</note> rise from any circle of Venus, the centers of the Octahedral faces penetrate and descend deeper below the outermost circle of Mercury, yet they do not reach the middle circle of Mercury (for he assigns three circles to every Planet, giving the Planet's eccentricity to the middle one). Finally, he claims that by the proportions of the Dodecahedral <note>twelve pentagonal faces</note> and Icosahedral <note>twenty triangular faces</note> orbs, being equal to each other, the proportions or intervals between the circles of Mars and the Earth, and between those of the Earth and Venus, are very close and equal to each other. In short, he makes the Cube and the Octahedron pairs, as well as the Dodecahedron and the Icosahedron. 

We ask, therefore—since the Author speaks so confidently that the number of planetary paths was chosen by the most-wise Creator from the five bodies—that he assign testimony for this fact from the sacred scriptures, since the fabric of the world is found most exactly within them. Upon these proportions of regular bodies, he seems to build the consonances of his harmony. Hence, a ratio of 1:2—that is, the *Diapason* <note>the interval of an octave</note> consonance—is attributed by him to Saturn and Jupiter, related to the cube. But he makes the proportions of the extreme motions of Saturn and Jupiter about 2:3, the *Diapente* <note>the interval of a perfect fifth</note>. To Venus and Mercury, he attributes a greater harmony of 1:4, because, just as the Cube is the first of the primary figures, so the Octahedron is the first of the secondary. And so on with the others. To these, he adds many rules coinciding with this fabricated doctrine, or rather, a doctrine that is analogical in the highest degree. It would be far too long to review all of these in this place. From these things, a wise man may see how far these inventions of the Author are from the true foundations of philosophy, which he promises with such effort and such exact order to follow from Nature; about which he is clear in his final comparison of harmonies with one another. Now indeed, we shall hasten to his discovery of concinnous <note>technically "well-fitting" or harmonious</note> intervals.

### TEXT XIII.

> Where I expressly refute the ancients when they discuss the causes of consonances, there he follows without a roll of the dice <note>original: "citra dubitationis aleam," meaning without hesitation or doubt</note>: he does not even think of truer causes.

### ANALYSIS.

This, then, is the controversy between him and the Ancients: namely, whether the smaller intervals—such as the Tone, the Semitone, and the Diesis <note>a very small interval, often less than a semitone</note> (which he calls "concinnous")—are simple elements from which, as if from causes or principles, the consonant intervals or major consonances arise. The Ancients affirm this; but *Johannes Kepler* denies it, and in *Chapter 4 of his 3rd book*, he attempts to refute their assertion with specific reasons. 

In reaching a solution to this doubt, he seems to propose this question: whether there is a minimum element common to all concinnous and consonant intervals, as a "simple" unit, from which any of the consonances and concinnous intervals might be composed by taking a certain number of them. This is what he utterly refuses to admit. The sole reason brought forward by him is of this kind: If the individuals of all species that differ in size consisted of one common minimum, there would then be some minimum quantity of the human species—some certain number of "tiny people" from which, as if from elements, any man would be composed: a tall man from many, a dwarf from few. For that quality in harmonics, *Consonance*, no less informs the proportion of the strings or the interval of the voices than the human form informs that mass of matter which is enclosed by human skin. Similarly, he cites a comparative reason from examples of incommensurable quantities in all types of Geometry, making a relationship between them and incommensurable intervals. 

From this, he infers that consonant intervals are prior in Nature to the smaller intervals, which he calls concinnous; and consequently, he argues that the former are not composed of the latter as if from elements, or from any smaller quantity, but rather the opposite. He says that he does not accept the quantitative division of a thing as its "composition" here, but rather its natural origin; just as the limbs of a man did not exist before the body, and the body was not collected from them like a house from stones. Accepting "composition" in this way, he denies that consonant intervals are composed either of other consonances or of concinnous intervals. He argues there that, although the causes of consonant intervals are related, they do not have the same cause, but rather different ones, insofar as each has its own distinct proper cause. And he concludes later...

He also critiques Kepler's musical theories, specifically Kepler's rejection of the idea that musical consonances are built out of smaller, "atomic" intervals like tones and semitones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s rebuttal to Johannes Kepler. Having previously discussed Kepler’s planetary model, Fludd now turns his attention to the mathematical and musical principles that Kepler used to support his theories. Fludd argues against Kepler’s view that musical intervals are derived from larger harmonies, asserting instead that they are built up from the most basic, "simple" units like the number one or a single point.</meta>

### TO THE KEPLERIAN APPENDIX

...afterward, because pleasing <term>concinnous; intervals that sound well together but are not primary harmonies</term> intervals arise from consonant <term>consonantia; primary musical harmonies like the octave or fifth</term> intervals, rather than consonants arising from the addition of pleasing ones. This, then, is the force of his arguments, by which he intends to prove that the smallest intervals flow in a natural order from the largest; and consequently, he teaches that they are by no means simple, but rather born from the simple. But I, adhering closely to the opinions of the ancients, will not hesitate to refute this opinion of his—first by the way of Arithmetic; then by reasons drawn from the principles of Geometry; and then finally by the very principles of natural things. Lastly, I shall turn the force of all my arguments toward the musical examination of consonance.

First, therefore, to draw the thread of my discourse to Arithmetic, we see that according to a certain order of nature, its **unity** <note>the number 1</note> is the common measure, source, and origin of all numbers, containing within itself every number, however large, and yet being devoid of all multiplicity—always the same and immutable. Hence, this unity multiplied by itself produces only itself: for it is its own root, square, and cube. It is therefore the beginning and the end of all things. From the progression of this unity, the number **two** <note>binarius</note> is produced in the first place, thus: **II**. In the second place, the number **three** <note>ternarius</note> is produced, thus: **III**. From this it is clear that the number **seven** <note>septenarius</note> (for from that many smaller or pleasing intervals the **Diapason** <note>original: "Diapason"; the interval of an octave, which consists of seven steps or intervals</note> consonance is forged, which is the most perfect consonance) will stand thus: **IIIIIII**. And so it is established from this that its origin—namely, the smallest element of the number seven from which it arises—is unity; and consequently, the same reasoning applies to the origin of the smaller consonances, namely those consisting of 3 or 4 intervals. 

For we do not say, following the order of nature, that **1** proceeds from the number seven as a beginning, nor from three, nor finally from four. For it is vain to imagine this in a natural progression toward generation—that is, in a progress from the prior to the posterior. But this can be said in the flow from composition back to the dissolution of the whole composite into its original parts, of which unity is the source; that is, in a regression from the posterior—from the point of the end-goal back to the Prior or the point of departure. 

We respond, therefore, to his earlier reasoning: that a formal physical unity, along with its material likeness as the smallest part, is the primary cause of an individual of the human species, which increases or decreases in its progression according to the size or smallness of its mass. As for the fact that one man's soul is more active and another's more dull, this does not happen on the part of the "form" <note>the soul</note>, which is always the same, but from a more fit or more sluggish preparation of "matter" <note>the body</note>. Thus, the same fire is observed to act differently in a damp medium than in a dry one; in a melancholic body than in a sanguine one; in iron than in wood; and yet it is always the same thing, and in its form is no more changed than the very source of light from which it proceeded. Similarly, against his assertion where he says that the causes of consonant intervals are related but not the same, it is clear from these points that in Arithmetical consideration, that smallest element is the original unity, which is the source of numbers and the origin of progression, proceeding from the starting point to the finish, both in concrete units (which are partly material and partly formal) and in abstract ones.

In Geometry, the proportion of progressions is also the same: namely, from a **point** to a **line**, from a line to a **triangle**, and from a triangle to other plane figures, from which one finally arrives at solid bodies. We say, therefore, that in Geometry, natural progression happens from points to lines, which are nothing other than a flow or continuation of points. From these it is certain that the point is in Geometry what unity is in Arithmetic. By their progression and multitude, a line or a **chord** <note>original: "chorda"; referring here to a musical string</note> is made; by the various divisions of which there arise different intervals varying in species from one another. And just as in the composition of a line, the line is known to be greater the longer the flow of points is from its starting point, so it is also clear that the larger the consonant intervals are, the more deeply they immerse themselves into multitude. 

But truly, it was not so wisely said (as it seems to me) that points are derived from lines: for this would be to extract principles from the things they created, which would surely happen against the order of nature. Similarly, to proceed to more manifest Geometrical units, we see that the constitutive parts of a triangle are lines; which lines, in turn, are usually distinguished into their divisions or roots. Therefore, just as that triangular figure <note>original: "trigonea"</note> is prior by nature—that is, the principle of all other Geometrical figures, from which as a principle the four-sided, five-sided, six-sided, and all other figures arise—so also all other figures are resolved back into it. 

Hence, just as **God**, the principle of all things, is compared to a **Point** by the Sages—from whom all things proceed and into whom all things return—so the **Trigon** <note>Triangle</note> is compared to the **Holy Trinity**. For the point is considered by itself and in itself without dimension; but the triangle denotes its properties in the measurement of the world. We can, therefore, most rightly compare the **Tone** <note>original: "Tonus"; the interval of a whole step</note> to a line. From lines the Triangle comes forth, which is the origin of all Geometrical figures. For, unless it were impossible to reach the **Diapente** <note>original: "Diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> by a larger or smaller part of the **Diatessaron** <note>original: "Diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth</note>, it would be no less impossible to reach a square <note>original: "Tetragonum"</note> without the mediation of a Triangle. 

From this it is evident that the Tone, related to the line, is the manifest principle of the consonant intervals, whose greater dimension is the **Diapason** <note>the octave</note>. After this progression, one arrives at the secondary ratio of composition; just as the hidden principle relates like a point to a line, in whose progression from the primordial point toward the lengths of the **Semitone**, the **Comma**, and the **Diesis** <note>micro-intervals or small tuning differences</note>, other insensible intervals are derived. These things being considered in a Geometrical manner, it is clear that the smaller or more "pleasing" intervals are simple; from the addition of which the larger consonant intervals are made...

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that musical and geometric structures are built up from the simplest possible units (unity and the point) rather than being subdivided from larger wholes. He uses the analogy of the Trinity and the Triangle to explain how complex harmonies and shapes originate from divine simplicity.</summary>

...sounds are produced, and not the reverse, as Johannes <note>Johannes Kepler</note> taught. But now we will proceed to a deeper speculation of his **Cube**. "The Cube," he says in Book 5, chapter 1, "is the outermost and most spacious, because it is original and holds the logic of the whole." And this seems to be the reason for his discovery in the production of <term>concinnous; pleasing musical intervals that sound well together</term> intervals from consonant ones: and yet he knows very well that the **Cube** originally flows from the **Square**, and the square from its root, which is the **line**. For who, versed in Geometry, ever doubted whether a mathematical body is composed of surfaces, as those are from lines, by the motion and order of nature? For from two things, namely light and water, by a double action the four elements were produced; from their duplication again, the **Cube**—or the composite of elements—came into the light. Whence Aristotle most correctly applied the Cube to the element of **earth**, which exists as more grave, ponderous, and composite.

We conclude therefore against his assertion: that, although consonant intervals arise from Geometry, it does not follow that concinnous intervals emanate from consonants. And furthermore, it is clear that the Author himself, contrary to his promise, does not proceed in a natural order to amend all things according to the laws of nature so that confusion might be avoided; as will appear more clearly from the following reason:

For we assert that, although the Cube may be called the "most spacious" and "original," this happens for a different reason than the one we are proposing, as will be declared below. From the pure fountain of Nature, we also elicit—by reason of the investigation into the mystery of sounds and the voice—that sound is a certain multiplication of the identity of sound proceeding from the "point from which" to the "point to which," so that from the starting point of the impulse to the goal where the voice tends, there is a progression from **unity to multitude**.

For example: In thunder, we see that the form producing the noise is a fiery force leaping from a cloud as if from the fountain of a moving soul, just as the voice of an animal proceeds from the tabernacle of its soul, or as the blast of a cannon from its mouth. In this, the fire is the moving form, the gunpowder is the matter receiving the force and violently impelling the medium, and the aerial spirit itself, torn and divided, gives the blast and the sound. For the air resists the fiery virtue because of its own humidity, and by this resistance, it suffers, and by its "suffering" <note>the physical reaction of the air</note>, it gives out a voice.

It follows, then, that in sound there is a numbering of the voice from the impelling form to the goal of hearing. This numbering or motion occurs through the chaining and flow of aerial atoms in a physical or concrete medium, no differently than a line is continued and measured by the flow of points in a mathematical medium.

But in truth, the "form" of the air itself is always the same and continuous with the <term>Anima Mundi; the Universal Soul or World Soul</term>; no part of which can truly be divided from its whole, although because of the diversity of the subject in which it dwells, it is said to differ in our eyes. For not even a single ray of the Sun is ever separated from its body: for the soul of the whole is unique, the same, and entirely indivisible in itself. This is the sentiment and opinion of all the wise.

But now, because this formal essence fills every part of the air, the air itself—if struck by a strong impulse—is accustomed to react and resist by the virtue of its hidden form. Thus, it suffers a "fissure" or resistance in one part, and the impelling force, by gradually reverberating, produces a sound, as can be seen in an Echo. Thus we see that a pipe produces a voice from breath alone, and the striking of the air made by a wooden stick likewise gives a sound. And although the soul or form of the air is indivisible, nevertheless the substance of the air (which is "subtle water") is divided into minute parts no differently than water (which is "thick air") is divided into drops; through these, sound is "numbered" in the air just as a line is numbered through points.

Hence it happens that, just as a very small body requires a very small use of the universal form, so also a tiny impulse gives a tiny and brief sound, because it urges only a small portion of the air; and vice versa. Furthermore, where there is greater resistance—either by reason of the thickness of the medium numbering the sound, or by reason of the thickness and bluntness of the substance striking the air—there the voice is rendered deeper (*gravior*). But where the medium is thinner and the striking substance is sharper (*acutior*), there the voice becomes more high-pitched.

Thus a sword penetrates the air more easily because of its edge than a stick, which is blunt, and consequently cannot penetrate the air as easily, and for this reason, it returns a deeper and lower sound. From this, it follows that just as a large stick produces a blunt and deep noise when striking the air, so small rods make sharper sounds because the medium, with its form, does not resist as much. By a similar logic, two rods of the same size, but unequal in length, will perform the same relative to each other as a large and a tiny rod compared in striking the air. The reason is that a thicker and blunter rod pushes the same proportion of air as a very small and subtle one; for the long rod has as much air to resist it because of its length as the short one has because of its thickness. Thus we see a thin but long string sound as deeply as a larger, blunter, and thicker string of lesser length, as we observe daily in the use of the <term>Barbitus; an ancient multi-stringed instrument similar to a lute</term>.

Having observed these things, we shall hasten toward the natural logic of finding consonances. By the aforementioned experience of the rods, it will be very easy to divine how the various **hammers of Pythagoras** <note>A famous legend where Pythagoras discovered musical ratios by listening to the weights of blacksmiths' hammers</note> produced sounds proportional to one another. The proportion of one in size or weight to another was as 12 to 6 (or 2 to 1); of another as 8 to 6; and a third as 9 to 6. Here are found the proportions of the double, the sesquitertian, and the sesquialter, constituting the <term>Diapason; an octave (2:1 ratio)</term>, the <term>Diatessaron; a perfect fourth (4:3 ratio)</term>, and the <term>Diapente; a perfect fifth (3:2 ratio)</term>.

From these it is clear that, since the material of the hammers was the same (namely, iron), the proportion of the difference was in the size, which in this respect was the same as the weight. We say, therefore, that the smaller hammer made a smaller fissure in the air, whence the air, compressed against the anvil, leaped back in reverberation, and in the manner of an echo gave out a sharper voice, because the opening or dimension of the instrument penetrating the air was...

<summary>Fludd argues that musical harmonies originate from simple physical principles—the way physical objects strike and "fissure" the air—rather than Kepler's complex geometrical "Cube." He explains the physics of sound through analogies of thunder, sticks of varying thickness, and Pythagoras's legendary blacksmith hammers.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Fludd, Johannes Kepler, Cube, Geometry, Anima Mundi, Acoustics, Pythagoras, Hammers, Diapason, Diatessaron, Diapente, musical ratios, physics of sound</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s technical and philosophical rebuttal to Johannes Kepler. Fludd is arguing that musical harmony is not just a mathematical abstraction (as he claims Kepler treats it) but is rooted in the physical reality of how objects—hammers, strings, and cannons—displace and "fissure" the air. He is building a bridge between the "point" of geometry and the "atom" or "drop" of physical matter.</meta>

# TO THE KEPLERIAN APPENDIX

...sharper than the others; the middle hammer also gave a deeper sound than the first, because it made a wider fissure in the air than the previous one; finally, the largest produced the deepest sound because it caused the air to gape wider and divided it with greater force. Furthermore, a larger hammer tends to penetrate the air more deeply by its weight than a smaller one, on account of its heavier action in the air. 

Indeed, this proportion in the perforation of the air is best discerned in a military cannon; for the wider its orifice is, the lower its sound, and the more deeply it penetrates into the aerial medium, and the sound is heard further away. That there is also a multiplication and resistance in the substance of the air during the production of a voice—that is, that a numbering and division occurs in infinite small portions, and that likewise the form of the air is continuous and not divisible—is evident in thunder. There, suddenly, because of the continuation of the visible fiery form in the cloud with that which is invisibly expanded everywhere through the air, the light of lightning is perceived without any resistance of matter; but because of the "numbering" of the air <note>The physical process of sound waves traveling through the medium, which Fludd views as a sequential 'counting' of air particles</note>, which requires time, the crash reaches the ears far more slowly. 

Just as we see in the natural composition of iron, or hammers, the addition of parts of iron by which they are reduced into one larger mass to achieve such consonant sounds, so indeed do consonant parts require smaller components for their perfection. For if that which creates something behaves in a certain way by the natural proportion of its composition, it is necessary that the thing created also behaves in that same way. For just as the dimension of the string is, so will be the proportion of the sounding air in all things; as the hammer is, so is the response of the aerial form to the impelling force; as the rod is, so is the voice. 

For the universal form of the air conforms itself to the resistance of the form of the instrument attacking it, and thus—with force resisting and attacking force—the moist medium <note>original: *medium humidum*; Fludd refers to air as a fluid or 'moist' medium that carries sound</note> suffering their forces (namely action and resistance or reaction) narrates them and clearly indicates them by the "mouth of nature" adapting itself to any proportion, not unlike a person speaking to another person, and the latter responding in that moment; they perceive each other's will unanimously by virtue of the resonating medium. 

But now, to approach the mystery of the consonance of strings, we say that their proportions in Music do not differ from those we have related before. For although the hammer and the cannon make a fissure in the air that is not long but wide—that is, not linear, but rather superficial <note>occupying a surface area rather than a single line</note>; nevertheless, as we see in Geometry, a parallelogram of this kind † — does not have more area or capacity than a square † : so also the length of a string will not occupy more in the dimension of air than the plane of a hammer or the roundness of a cannon. 

Furthermore, this is the reason why between two strings, of which one is very thick and the other thin and small, that thick string—on account of its wide dominion in the air—sounds as deep as that small string stretched to triple or double its length, even though both strings are extended to the same measure. For example: 

,,,,,,,,,,,,,,, <note>representing the thick, short string</note> 
& 
—————— <note>representing the thin, long string</note> 

This happens for the reason mentioned above; namely because as the string is, so also is the fissure in the air. Whence a wide fissure that is not so long will equal one that is thinner in the same air but much longer. 

Having perceived these things in the physical consideration of sounds, I descend to the origin of consonances. Just as we said measurement is made in a mathematical line from point to point from the beginning of the line to its end, so also the reason is similar and the same in a physical line, which behaves differently than the mathematical one; since the latter is imaginary and in the abstract, while the former is real and in the concrete. 

For by the crash made by thunder, we have declared how the real numbering of motion occurs from place to place—or from the cloud to the earth—that is, the multiplication of real or physical units in the air from the starting point <note>original: *termino à quo*</note>, before the voice passes to the ears of the listener. We have said that the "form" carrying the sound is that universal and most hidden essence of the **world-sea** <note>original: *maris mundani*; Fludd's term for the primordial fluid or ether that fills the universe</note> which is never divided by itself, although it seems to be divided according to the division of the matter in which it exists. 

We say, therefore, that in any sound made in the air there is a triple motion: namely, in length, width, and depth. The figural proportion of the agitated instrument creates length or width; but the depth is adapted by the power of the impelling form; if it is greater and more vehement, it penetrates deeper; if smaller, more superficially. In each of these motions, a progression occurs from a single Physical Unit <note>original: *Physica*; the basic physical building block</note>, which is the starting point. But the strike in length and width is the cause of the proportion of consonances; its depth, however, is the cause of either a longer continuation of the sound, or that the sound is rendered more sonorous and distinct. Since, therefore, it is certain that there is no real line in which a Geometric surface is not found, it is clear that every material string has a parallelogram (either larger or smaller) within itself according to its greater or lesser density.

Whence it is manifest that, just as surfaces in Geometry have their origin from lines, and the first parallelogram from triangles, and then afterwards from lines, from which every square is generated in mathematical order; so also all those motions concurring for the natural production of the voice, and designating such physical figures and lines, take their principle from prior units, from which any motion flowed as if from a starting point. Since, therefore, every proportion of intervals is measured by motion, and all motion is measured by number, and every physical number is a real unity; just as a drop is held as the unit of water, and air is distinguished into atoms as its smallest quantities (as a line is by points); then also water has its lines from the chaining together of drops, and air from the progressive congregation of atoms; from which again surfaces and bodies compacted from them—that is, physical triple dimension—emerge. Thus, therefore, we say that in every striking of the air, motion occurs; because from the lesser...

<summary>Fludd argues that sound is a physical displacement of air, where the size and shape of the instrument (hammers, cannons, or strings) determine the pitch through "fissures" in the aerial medium. He concludes that musical intervals are rooted in physical atoms and units of motion, just as geometry is built from points and lines.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument against Johannes Kepler. Fludd is transitioning from the physical mechanics of sound (how hammers and cannons displace air) to a broader philosophical argument about how harmony "grows" from simple parts to complex wholes, much like biological life.</meta>

# ANALYTICAL DISCOURSE

...from a multitude, and one proceeds as if from a single smallest element until, through multiplication, a progression is made to a massive number. In that motion, therefore, from which a consonance <note>A harmonious sounding together of notes</note> is produced, let there be such a measure of motion in the air as there is in the substance of the string that generates that motion. Indeed, that string, in its natural growth from smaller to larger, had its progression within the belly of an animal <note>Fludd refers to the gut strings used in instruments like the lute, which grew as the animal grew</note>; and thus we also perceive hemp growing higher every day; and so every progression in generation occurs from the smaller to the larger. Why then should it be doubted that in consonances, when viewed through physical consideration, the progression happens from the smaller to the larger, rather than from the larger to the smaller? 

We say, therefore, that in the measurement of the **Diapason** <term>the octave</term>, **Diapente** <term>the perfect fifth</term>, and **Diatessaron** <term>the perfect fourth</term>, the motion in the air must be considered from the starting point to the end point: that is, from the beginning of a Tone to the end of the measurement of five whole tones with two Semitones to complete the **Diapason** consonance. In this motion, the transition first occurs through all the fitting intervals—through the **Schisma**, the **Comma**, the major and minor **Semitone** <note>These are technical terms for tiny mathematical intervals between notes</note>—before reaching the completion of a single Tone. And thus, by the multiplication of the air, one proceeds toward the goal of a perfect consonance. 

In this motion, although the air is divided, nevertheless the **form** acting within it is always the same, proportionally adapting itself to the larger consonance as well as the smaller. For it is ready to act in proportion to the part of the medium or its vehicle, which is urged to undergo the action. Furthermore, do we not see among the Physicists that all things are forged from their principles as if from parts, and not as smaller things from larger ones? But Johannes <note>Johannes Kepler</note> says that the internal cause of every consonance is diverse and distinct. This assertion of his is indirect, since the same acting form is in one proportion as in another—hardly different than how the same breath of a flute-player is accustomed to produce various consonances in a pipe. 

Nothing is less consistent with the truth than the idea that the **Diapason** consonance—which, on account of its extreme perfection, is compared to God himself—could be the principle of either imperfect consonances or incomplete intervals, which are even more imperfect when considered against an incomplete consonance. For who, being consistent with himself, would attempt to elicit the imperfect from that which is more than perfect? Either the **Diapason** consonance is completely perfect, and then it has nothing of imperfection in itself; or it is not completely perfect, and then it is wrongly called "perfection" in a higher degree. 

From these things, therefore, it is clear that just as maturity proceeds from rawness, so perfection proceeds from imperfection. For this is the continuous action of nature: to lead imperfect things to the goal of perfection. Indeed, the true progression of nature is to take a dark thing, defiled by raw shadows, and render it pure, formal, and lucid like the Sun itself. For he who knows how to elicit a Sun clearer from the imperfect than this celestial Sun is, he will perfectly understand what the truest **Mundane Harmony** <note>The "Music of the Spheres" or the inherent harmony of the universe</note> of the **Diapason** is; he will look with open eyes from imperfection to the goal of perfection, and he will find the numerical progression from formal unity into the multitude of matter, and the reduction of that same matter from multitude back to unity. For so was the world made: namely, from one into a multitude; and so also by transfiguration it will be changed at the end, so that "it perishes back into one" <note>original: "Pereat ad unum"; a reference to the alchemical or philosophical return of all creation to the divine Monad</note>. Such a person, therefore, will truly best deserve the name of Musician; and such a person will obtain a clear insight into divine Astronomy, the most certain of all; to him neither the positions of the stars, nor their motions, nor the reason of the seasons, nor the order of times will be hidden, but with the spirit of wisdom as his guide, he will be directed on a certain and infallible path of knowledge.

### TEXT XIV

->*Let us pass to another passage of the author, where he introduces Music into the world. Here, indeed, there is a massive difference between us. First, the harmonies he intends to teach are mere Symbolisms; regarding these, I say the same as I do of the symbols of Ptolemy: they are poetic or oratorical rather than Philosophical or mathematical.*<-

### ANALYSIS

This will not (I hope) be a disgrace to me, to have fallen into the same error as Ptolemy himself, a man so very famous; rather, it shall be turned into an honor that I have thought something similar to Ptolemy. Suppose it is granted that these are "mere Poetic symbolisms"; nevertheless, we utterly deny that we have wandered from the threshold of the Philosophers for that reason. For I see that the Author <note>Kepler</note> is unaware of how much mystery the symbols of Orpheus, Homer, Hesiod, Virgil, Ovid, and others like them contain within themselves. But, to come to his own doctrine: were not his "regular bodies" <note>The five Platonic solids, which Kepler used to model the orbits of the planets</note>, which he established as the foundations of his harmonic work, mere symbols? These Pythagoras himself adapted to true philosophy, drawing from them great mysteries about the changes of the elements from imperfection to perfection. Yet the Author, dragging these symbols away from the intention of true philosophy toward his own Astronomical fictions, made them vain and truly Poetic, or mathematical ravings.

<summary>Fludd argues that harmony and perfection grow from simple, imperfect parts (like an embryo or a plant), rejecting Kepler's view that perfect musical ratios are primary. He defends his use of "symbolism" by comparing himself to Ptolemy and accusing Kepler's own astronomical models of being "mathematical ravings."</summary>
